They want like two hundred bucks just to show up at the door! And don't even get me started on the price of Romex wire right now. I went to buy a 50-foot roll of 12/2 wire for that circuit and nearly had a heart attack right there in the aisle. It's like they think copper is made of gold these days. I ended up having to splice into the old junction box, which I hate doing, because you never know how the last guy pig-tailed the grounds, but... \n\nWait, what were you asking? Oh, if the spot is really there. I mean, yeah, I see it. Even if they don't, I know what I'm looking at. But I don't know, I guess maybe I just stare at it too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face isn't actually there, even if you see it?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. Maybe? I mean, I guess it's possible, since literally everyone else tells me I'm nuts. But it feels real to me. \n\nKind of like how my dog acts when the mailman comes by. We got this rescue a couple years ago, part shepherd, part who-knows-what. Great dog, mostly, but he goes absolutely ballistic every time the mail truck pulls up. He's convinced that guy is a threat, right? Barks his head off, jumping at the window. And I’m always telling him, \"Buddy, it's just Gary, he's bringing us junk mail and bills, he's not gonna hurt you.\" But to the dog, the threat is 100% real. He can't unsee it. \n\nWhich reminds me, I really need to fix the screen on that front window before summer. He clawed right through it last week when a squirrel ran across the porch railing. Those replacement screens aren't cheap either, and you have to get that little spline roller tool to push the rubber cord in... I tried doing it with a flathead screwdriver once and just ripped a hole right through the brand new mesh. So frustrating. \n\nBut yeah... what was the question? Oh, right. Is it possible it's not there. I mean, I suppose it's possible. It just doesn't feel that way when I'm looking right at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the sunken patch on your face at times when you're not looking in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. It's not just when I'm looking in the mirror. I mean, that's why I'm here, honestly. I just can't stop thinking about it. Even when I'm not looking at it, I'm picturing it. Like, I'll see a photo of myself and it's all I can focus on—this rough, sunken-looking patch that makes one side of my face look all old and beat-up. My wife and the guys at work keep telling me they barely notice anything, but it's always on my mind. \n\nIt actually distracted me at work the other day. We were pouring concrete for this new patio behind a house over in Westlake. You know, you really gotta pay attention when you're floating concrete, especially when the mix is a little wet and the sun is beating down on it. If you don't hit it with the bull float at the right time, the water bleeds to the top and you get this weak surface that's gonna flake off after the first freeze. Anyway, we had the truck backed right up to the forms, and the chute was swinging around... and I swear, I caught my reflection in the side mirror of the cement mixer. Just for a second. But it completely threw me off. \n\nI ended up stepping right into the wet pour. Ruined a good pair of boots, too. Those Red Wings aren't cheap, I've had them for three years and just got them resoled. The guy at the cobbler shop downtown did a great job, though. He used this heavy-duty Vibram sole, stitched it right on... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. It's always in the back of my head. Just picturing that beat-up spot on my face, even when I'm miles away from a mirror.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the sunken patch on your face affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. I mean, my wife is getting pretty fed up with me. I've been late getting out the door for work so many times because I'm just stuck in the bathroom, trying to fix how it looks. And you can't be late on a job site. The foreman, Dave, he's a real stickler for time. If you're not there by 6:00 AM sharp, ready to pull tools out of the truck, he'll just send you home for the day. No pay. And Dave's been on edge lately anyway because his truck's transmission is slipping. He's got this old Ford F-250, diesel, and he refuses to take it to a real mechanic. Says he can drop the pan and fix the valve body himself, but he's been saying that for six months. I keep telling him, \"Dave, once those clutch packs go, you're looking at a full rebuild.\" But he won't listen. \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, right, my family and stuff. Yeah, I've started dodging the site photos when the boss wants a picture of the crew in front of a finished pour. I just kind of step out of frame or pretend I need to grab something from the truck. And I've been skipping our family game nights on Fridays, too. My kids love playing Catan, but I just... I don't want to sit around the table under those bright lights with everyone looking at me. They think I'm just tired from work, but really I just don't want them staring at my face.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your work performance due to your concerns about the sunken patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying, it's definitely messing with work. I've been late getting out the door for jobs because I get stuck in the bathroom, just staring at it, trying to figure out if there's a way to hide it. And like I said, Dave doesn't mess around with guys being late. \n\nWhich, honestly, is ironic because he was late just yesterday. That transmission of his finally gave out on him on the highway. He had to get it towed all the way from the east side. The tow truck driver actually got lost, ended up in this residential neighborhood where the streets are too narrow, and he clipped somebody's mailbox. I guess it was one of those fancy brick ones, too, so that's gonna cost a fortune to fix. Dave was furious. He was pacing around the job site all morning, just chewing on a cigar he wasn't even smoking. \n\nBut, uh, yeah. Work. I've been dodging the site photos, too, like I mentioned. Whenever the boss wants a group shot of the crew in front of a finished pour, I just find an excuse to be somewhere else. I just don't want that patch on my face immortalized in some picture they're gonna put on the company Facebook page, you know? And it's not just work, it's home, too. Skipping those family game nights... I just don't want anyone looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the sunken patch on your face caused you to avoid social interactions or activities that you would normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I kind of already touched on that, didn't I? Like I was saying, I've been skipping out on our Friday family game nights. I just don't want to sit under that bright dining room chandelier with them staring at my face. We usually play Catan, or sometimes Monopoly. Though Monopoly always ends in an argument anyway because my youngest son always goes straight for the railroads. People think the expensive blue properties are the big ticket, but honestly, if you get all four railroads and the orange ones, you're set. Statistically, people land on the orange ones the most because of where they are in relation to the jail space. I actually read this whole article about it in a magazine while I was waiting at the dentist a few years ago. Dr. Henderson, great guy. He retired last spring, though, and sold his practice to this young kid right out of dental school who tried to tell me I needed an expensive night guard... \n\nAnyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, avoiding things. Yeah, I skip the game nights now. And like I mentioned before, I'm still dodging those site photos at work, and I'm running late in the mornings getting out the door for jobs because I'm stuck in the mirror. I just can't stand the idea of people looking at that sunken patch, so I just try to keep out of the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concern about the sunken patch on your face is affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it is. I mean, it's hard to be happy when you're just constantly worried about it. Like I've been saying, it's messing with everything. I'm late for jobs because I'm stuck in the bathroom, I'm dodging the crew photos, and I'm skipping game nights with the kids. It just... it wears you down. \n\nAnd speaking of wearing down, my work boots are completely shot. I've had these Red Wings for about four years now, and the tread is just gone. I almost slipped on a patch of wet concrete the other day. You gotta be careful with wet concrete, too, because if it gets on your skin and you don't wash it off right away, it'll give you chemical burns. We had a new guy last summer, barely eighteen, who didn't listen when we told him to wear long pants. He ended up with these nasty red welts all over his shins. Boss had to send him to the clinic. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. It just feels like this whole thing with my face is taking over. I'm just so tired of trying to hide it all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your perception of the sunken patch on your face might be different from how it actually looks to others?\n\nPatient: I mean... yeah, I guess so. People keep telling me that, anyway. My wife, the guys at the site, they all keep saying they barely notice anything at all. But I just don't get how they can't see it. I look in the mirror, or I look at those photos, and I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch. It makes that whole side of me look so old and beat-up. \n\nSpeaking of looking old, I actually thought maybe it was just bad lighting casting a weird shadow, so I tried changing the bulbs in our bathroom vanity last weekend. I went down to Home Depot to get those soft white ones, but the lighting aisle is a total mess now. It's all LEDs. You can't even find a normal incandescent bulb anymore because of those new energy regulations. I get wanting to save electricity, I really do, but those LEDs give off this weird, harsh light, like you're in a hospital or a meat cooler or something. I spent twenty minutes just staring at the boxes trying to figure out the lumens and the kelvins. The guy working there, kid with a green apron, didn't know anything either. He was just stocking the dimmer switches and looking at his phone. \n\nBut anyway... what was I saying? Oh, right. Even with the new bulbs, I still see it. It's just right there, making my face look all caved in, even if everyone else swears they have no idea what I'm talking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is only visible to you?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know how it could be *only* visible to me. It's right there. Like I said, I'm here because I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face. Every time I look in the mirror, or if I accidentally catch a glimpse of myself in one of those photos, it's all I can focus on. It makes that whole side of my face look so old and beat-up. \n\nIt's like when you buy a used truck, you know? My buddy Dave just bought this '08 Chevy Silverado. He thought he got a steal on it, but the guy who sold it to him painted over a massive rust spot on the rear quarter panel. Dave didn't notice it until it rained a few days later and the cheap paint started flaking off. Now he's got this huge rusted-out crater on the side of his truck, and he's furious. He's talking about taking the guy to small claims court, but I told him, \"Dave, you bought it as-is off Craigslist. Good luck with that.\" You really have to check the undercarriage, too, when you buy used. People forget that, but that's where the salt from the roads really eats away at the frame during the winter. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... the patch on my face. It's like that rust spot, you know? It's just glaring at me. Even though my wife and the guys at work keep saying they barely notice anything, I just don't understand how they can't see it. It's right there. I see it every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something that only you can see, and it's not actually there at all?\n\nPatient: Not there at all? I just... I don't see how that's possible. It's not like I'm imagining things. It's right there on my skin. It's not a mirage. \n\nYou know, speaking of mirages, it gets exactly like that when we do road work in the dead of summer. The asphalt gets so hot it literally looks like there are puddles of water on the highway ahead of you. We were doing this long stretch of I-95 a couple years back, and the heat index was pushing a hundred and ten. You have to drink those electrolyte powders they hand out, or you will absolutely drop. We had this foreman, Gary, stubborn old guy who insisted he only needed black coffee and cigarettes to get through a twelve-hour shift. Well, around two in the afternoon, he just keeled over right next to the steam roller. Scared the absolute hell out of us. The paramedics said his core temp was dangerously high. Ever since then, the safety coordinator makes us take mandatory water and shade breaks every hour when it gets above ninety degrees. It slows down the whole project, but I guess it beats having guys pass out on the blacktop. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I am not hallucinating this thing on my face. It is real. Even if everyone else is blind to it or just trying to make me feel better, I know what I am looking at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something you're seeing because of your own worries, rather than something that's actually there?\n\nPatient: Look, Doc, I feel like a broken record here. It's not just my anxiety playing tricks on me. Like I keep trying to explain, the whole reason I'm even sitting in this chair is because I cannot stop obsessing over that rough, sunken spot. I look in the mirror, or I see a picture somebody took, and boom—there it is. Half my face just looks completely old and beat-up. I know my wife and the crew keep saying they barely notice a thing, but I'm not making it up from stress. \n\nIf my brain was gonna make me see things just from worrying, I'd be hallucinating about the price of building materials right now. You want to talk about worries, you should see the cost of plywood these days. It's highway robbery. We were framing out this custom build last month, and the supplier bumped the quote up by twenty percent overnight. The general contractor, Jimmy, he nearly blew a gasket. He was on the phone screaming at the rep for an hour straight. And don't even get me started on copper piping. Guys are breaking into active job sites just to strip the copper out of the walls and sell it for scrap. We had to hire a night watchman for a site downtown because they hit us three times in one week. Guy just sits in his truck drinking Mountain Dew all night, but I guess it keeps the thieves away. It's a crazy world right now. \n\nBut anyway... no, it's not my worries making the patch appear. I'm telling you, I can't stop seeing it. That patch is right there on my skin, making me look all beat-up, even if nobody else wants to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something that isn't actually there, but rather a product of your own mind?\n\nPatient: Doc, are we going in circles here? I feel like you're asking me the exact same thing again. Look, I know I've said it a hundred times, but it's not a product of my mind. I'm literally sitting in this office because I cannot stop looking at it. Every single time I pass a mirror or see a photo, my eyes go straight to that rough, sunken patch. It just makes that whole side of my face look so old and beat-up. I know my wife and the guys at the site swear they barely notice a thing, but I'm not inventing it. \n\nSpeaking of things that are just in people's minds, though, you should see some of the blueprints these architects send down to us. They sit in their air-conditioned offices and dream up these crazy designs that look great in their heads, but when you actually try to build it in the real world, the physics just don't work. We had this one guy last spring who wanted a solid marble kitchen island suspended from the ceiling with these thin little wire cables. Suspended! I told the GC, \"Unless you've got anti-gravity in the budget, that slab is coming straight down on someone's toes.\" We ended up having to weld this massive steel support structure hidden inside a fake drywall column just to make the guy's \"vision\" happen. Took an extra three weeks and cost the client an absolute fortune just because the architect wouldn't let go of this picture he had in his head. \n\nBut yeah... my face isn't like that. It's not some crazy imagination thing. It's real, and I can't stop seeing it, no matter how many times you ask me if it's all in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It's real, and I can't stop seeing it, no matter how many times you ask me if it's all in my head.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still see the sunken patch on your face if a doctor or a dermatologist told you there was nothing wrong with your skin?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 52 / construction_laborer / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I honestly don't know. I haven't been to a skin doctor about it, so I'm not really sure what I'd do if they said that. I'd probably just think they needed to get their eyes checked, because I mean, I'm looking right at it in the mirror every day. 

Speaking of skin doctors, though, my buddy Dave had to go to one last year. We were doing this massive gut rehab on a house from the seventies over in Westlake, and the attic was just packed to the rafters with that old, pink fiberglass insulation. Just nasty, itchy stuff. Well, Dave didn't wear his long sleeves like he was supposed to—he always runs hot, you know, sweats like a pig even in November. Anyway, he gets this fiberglass dust all in his pores, and his arms broke out in this insane, angry red rash. Looked like raw hamburger meat. He tried scrubbing it off in the utility sink with pumice soap, which obviously just made it ten times worse. He finally had to go to the dermatologist, and they gave him this heavy-duty steroid cream. Cost him like eighty bucks out of pocket even with the union insurance. Now the guy won't go near an attic without a full Tyvek suit. He looks like he's about to walk on the moon just to pull some drywall. 

But, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, if a doctor told me my face was fine. Yeah, I don't know. I'd probably just think they were blowing smoke, honestly. Because I can see it.

We had this foreman, Gary, stubborn old guy who insisted he only needed black coffee and cigarettes to get through a twelve-hour shift. Well, around two in the afternoon, he just keeled over right next to the steam roller. Scared the absolute hell out of us. The paramedics said his core temp was dangerously high. Ever since then, the safety coordinator makes us take mandatory water and shade breaks every hour when it gets above ninety degrees. It slows down the whole project, but I guess it beats having guys pass out on the blacktop. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I am not hallucinating this thing on my face. It is real. It's not just my anxiety playing tricks on me. Like I keep trying to explain, the whole reason I'm even sitting in this chair is because I cannot stop obsessing over that rough, sunken spot. I look in the mirror, or I see a picture somebody took, and boom—there it is. Half my face just looks completely old and beat-up. I know my wife and the crew keep saying they barely notice a thing, but I'm not making it up from stress. \n\nIf my brain was gonna make me see things just from worrying, I'd be hallucinating about the price of building materials right now. We were framing out this custom build last month, and the supplier bumped the quote up by twenty percent overnight. The general contractor, Jimmy, he nearly blew a gasket. He was on the phone screaming at the rep for an hour straight. And don't even get me started on copper piping. Guys are breaking into active job sites just to strip the copper out of the walls and sell it for scrap. We had to hire a night watchman for a site downtown because they hit us three times in one week. Guy just sits in his truck drinking Mountain Dew all night, but I guess it keeps the thieves away. It's a crazy world right now. \n\nBut anyway... no, it's not my worries making the patch appear. I'm telling you, I can't stop seeing it. That patch is right there on my skin, making me look all beat-up, even if nobody else wants to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something that isn't actually there, but rather a product of your own mind?\n\nPatient: Doc, are we going in circles here? I feel like you're asking me the exact same thing again. Look, I know I've said it a hundred times, but it's not a product of my mind. I'm literally sitting in this office because I cannot stop looking at it. Every single time I pass a mirror or see a photo, my eyes go straight to that rough, sunken patch. It just makes that whole side of my face look so old and beat-up. They sit in their air-conditioned offices and dream up these crazy designs that look great in their heads, but when you actually try to build it in the real world, the physics just don't work. We had this one guy last spring who wanted a solid marble kitchen island suspended from the ceiling with these thin little wire cables. Suspended! I told the GC, \"Unless you've got anti-gravity in the budget, that slab is coming straight down on someone's toes.\" We ended up having to weld this massive steel support structure hidden inside a fake drywall column just to make the guy's \"vision\" happen. Took an extra three weeks and cost the client an absolute fortune just because the architect wouldn't let go of this picture he had in his head. \n\nBut yeah... my face isn't like that. It's not some crazy imagination thing. It's real, and I can't stop seeing it, no matter how many times you ask me if it's all in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see the sunken patch on your face if a doctor or a dermatologist told you there was nothing wrong with your skin?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Oh, if the spot is really there. I mean, yeah, I see it. Even if they don't, I know what I'm looking at. But I don't know, I guess maybe I just stare at it too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face isn't actually there, even if you see it?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. Maybe? I mean, I guess it's possible, since literally everyone else tells me I'm nuts. But it feels real to me. \n\nKind of like how my dog acts when the mailman comes by. We got this rescue a couple years ago, part shepherd, part who-knows-what. Great dog, mostly, but he goes absolutely ballistic every time the mail truck pulls up. He's convinced that guy is a threat, right? Barks his head off, jumping at the window. And I’m always telling him, \"Buddy, it's just Gary, he's bringing us junk mail and bills, he's not gonna hurt you.\" But to the dog, the threat is 100% real. He can't unsee it. \n\nWhich reminds me, I really need to fix the screen on that front window before summer. He clawed right through it last week when a squirrel ran across the porch railing. Those replacement screens aren't cheap either, and you have to get that little spline roller tool to push the rubber cord in... I tried doing it with a flathead screwdriver once and just ripped a hole right through the brand new mesh. So frustrating. \n\nBut yeah... what was the question? Oh, right. Is it possible it's not there. I mean, I suppose it's possible. It just doesn't feel that way when I'm looking right at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the sunken patch on your face at times when you're not looking in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. It's not just when I'm looking in the mirror. I mean, that's why I'm here, honestly. I just can't stop thinking about it. Even when I'm not looking at it, I'm picturing it. Like, I'll see a photo of myself and it's all I can focus on—this rough, sunken-looking patch that makes one side of my face look all old and beat-up. My wife and the guys at work keep telling me they barely notice anything, but it's always on my mind. \n\nIt actually distracted me at work the other day. We were pouring concrete for this new patio behind a house over in Westlake. You know, you really gotta pay attention when you're floating concrete, especially when the mix is a little wet and the sun is beating down on it. If you don't hit it with the bull float at the right time, the water bleeds to the top and you get this weak surface that's gonna flake off after the first freeze. Anyway, we had the truck backed right up to the forms, and the chute was swinging around... and I swear, I caught my reflection in the side mirror of the cement mixer. Just for a second. But it completely threw me off. \n\nI ended up stepping right into the wet pour. Ruined a good pair of boots, too. Those Red Wings aren't cheap, I've had them for three years and just got them resoled. The guy at the cobbler shop downtown did a great job, though. He used this heavy-duty Vibram sole, stitched it right on... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. It's always in the back of my head. Just picturing that beat-up spot on my face, even when I'm miles away from a mirror.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the sunken patch on your face affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. I mean, my wife is getting pretty fed up with me. I've been late getting out the door for work so many times because I'm just stuck in the bathroom, trying to fix how it looks. And you can't be late on a job site. The foreman, Dave, he's a real stickler for time. If you're not there by 6:00 AM sharp, ready to pull tools out of the truck, he'll just send you home for the day. No pay. And Dave's been on edge lately anyway because his truck's transmission is slipping. He's got this old Ford F-250, diesel, and he refuses to take it to a real mechanic. Says he can drop the pan and fix the valve body himself, but he's been saying that for six months. I keep telling him, \"Dave, once those clutch packs go, you're looking at a full rebuild.\" But he won't listen. \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, right, my family and stuff. Yeah, I've started dodging the site photos when the boss wants a picture of the crew in front of a finished pour. I just kind of step out of frame or pretend I need to grab something from the truck. And I've been skipping our family game nights on Fridays, too. My kids love playing Catan, but I just... I don't want to sit around the table under those bright lights with everyone looking at me. They think I'm just tired from work, but really I just don't want them staring at my face.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your work performance due to your concerns about the sunken patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying, it's definitely messing with work. I've been late getting out the door for jobs because I get stuck in the bathroom, just staring at it, trying to figure out if there's a way to hide it. And like I said, Dave doesn't mess around with guys being late. \n\nWhich, honestly, is ironic because he was late just yesterday. That transmission of his finally gave out on him on the highway. He had to get it towed all the way from the east side. The tow truck driver actually got lost, ended up in this residential neighborhood where the streets are too narrow, and he clipped somebody's mailbox. I guess it was one of those fancy brick ones, too, so that's gonna cost a fortune to fix. Dave was furious. He was pacing around the job site all morning, just chewing on a cigar he wasn't even smoking. \n\nBut, uh, yeah. Work. I've been dodging the site photos, too, like I mentioned. Whenever the boss wants a group shot of the crew in front of a finished pour, I just find an excuse to be somewhere else. I just don't want that patch on my face immortalized in some picture they're gonna put on the company Facebook page, you know? And it's not just work, it's home, too. Skipping those family game nights... I just don't want anyone looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the sunken patch on your face caused you to avoid social interactions or activities that you would normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I kind of already touched on that, didn't I? Like I was saying, I've been skipping out on our Friday family game nights. I just don't want to sit under that bright dining room chandelier with them staring at my face. We usually play Catan, or sometimes Monopoly. Though Monopoly always ends in an argument anyway because my youngest son always goes straight for the railroads. People think the expensive blue properties are the big ticket, but honestly, if you get all four railroads and the orange ones, you're set. Statistically, people land on the orange ones the most because of where they are in relation to the jail space. I actually read this whole article about it in a magazine while I was waiting at the dentist a few years ago. Dr. Henderson, great guy. He retired last spring, though, and sold his practice to this young kid right out of dental school who tried to tell me I needed an expensive night guard... \n\nAnyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, avoiding things. Yeah, I skip the game nights now. And like I mentioned before, I'm still dodging those site photos at work, and I'm running late in the mornings getting out the door for jobs because I'm stuck in the mirror. I just can't stand the idea of people looking at that sunken patch, so I just try to keep out of the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concern about the sunken patch on your face is affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it is. I mean, it's hard to be happy when you're just constantly worried about it. Like I've been saying, it's messing with everything. I'm late for jobs because I'm stuck in the bathroom, I'm dodging the crew photos, and I'm skipping game nights with the kids. It just... it wears you down. \n\nAnd speaking of wearing down, my work boots are completely shot. I've had these Red Wings for about four years now, and the tread is just gone. I almost slipped on a patch of wet concrete the other day. You gotta be careful with wet concrete, too, because if it gets on your skin and you don't wash it off right away, it'll give you chemical burns. We had a new guy last summer, barely eighteen, who didn't listen when we told him to wear long pants. He ended up with these nasty red welts all over his shins. Boss had to send him to the clinic. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. It just feels like this whole thing with my face is taking over. I'm just so tired of trying to hide it all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your perception of the sunken patch on your face might be different from how it actually looks to others?\n\nPatient: I mean... yeah, I guess so. People keep telling me that, anyway. My wife, the guys at the site, they all keep saying they barely notice anything at all. But I just don't get how they can't see it. I look in the mirror, or I look at those photos, and I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch. It makes that whole side of me look so old and beat-up. \n\nSpeaking of looking old, I actually thought maybe it was just bad lighting casting a weird shadow, so I tried changing the bulbs in our bathroom vanity last weekend. I went down to Home Depot to get those soft white ones, but the lighting aisle is a total mess now. It's all LEDs. You can't even find a normal incandescent bulb anymore because of those new energy regulations. I get wanting to save electricity, I really do, but those LEDs give off this weird, harsh light, like you're in a hospital or a meat cooler or something. I spent twenty minutes just staring at the boxes trying to figure out the lumens and the kelvins. The guy working there, kid with a green apron, didn't know anything either. He was just stocking the dimmer switches and looking at his phone. \n\nBut anyway... what was I saying? Oh, right. Even with the new bulbs, I still see it. It's just right there, making my face look all caved in, even if everyone else swears they have no idea what I'm talking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is only visible to you?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know how it could be *only* visible to me. It's right there. Like I said, I'm here because I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face. Every time I look in the mirror, or if I accidentally catch a glimpse of myself in one of those photos, it's all I can focus on. It makes that whole side of my face look so old and beat-up. \n\nIt's like when you buy a used truck, you know? My buddy Dave just bought this '08 Chevy Silverado. He thought he got a steal on it, but the guy who sold it to him painted over a massive rust spot on the rear quarter panel. Dave didn't notice it until it rained a few days later and the cheap paint started flaking off. Now he's got this huge rusted-out crater on the side of his truck, and he's furious. He's talking about taking the guy to small claims court, but I told him, \"Dave, you bought it as-is off Craigslist. Good luck with that.\" You really have to check the undercarriage, too, when you buy used. People forget that, but that's where the salt from the roads really eats away at the frame during the winter. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... the patch on my face. It's like that rust spot, you know? It's just glaring at me. Even though my wife and the guys at work keep saying they barely notice anything, I just don't understand how they can't see it. It's right there. I see it every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something that only you can see, and it's not actually there at all?\n\nPatient: Not there at all? I just... I don't see how that's possible. It's not like I'm imagining things. It's right there on my skin. It's not a mirage. \n\nYou know, speaking of mirages, it gets exactly like that when we do road work in the dead of summer. The asphalt gets so hot it literally looks like there are puddles of water on the highway ahead of you. We were doing this long stretch of I-95 a couple years back, and the heat index was pushing a hundred and ten. You have to drink those electrolyte powders they hand out, or you will absolutely drop. We had this foreman, Gary, stubborn old guy who insisted he only needed black coffee and cigarettes to get through a twelve-hour shift. Well, around two in the afternoon, he just keeled over right next to the steam roller. Scared the absolute hell out of us. The paramedics said his core temp was dangerously high. Ever since then, the safety coordinator makes us take mandatory water and shade breaks every hour when it gets above ninety degrees. It slows down the whole project, but I guess it beats having guys pass out on the blacktop. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I am not hallucinating this thing on my face. It is real. Even if everyone else is blind to it or just trying to make me feel better, I know what I am looking at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something you're seeing because of your own worries, rather than something that's actually there?\n\nPatient: Look, Doc, I feel like a broken record here. It's not just my anxiety playing tricks on me. Like I keep trying to explain, the whole reason I'm even sitting in this chair is because I cannot stop obsessing over that rough, sunken spot. I look in the mirror, or I see a picture somebody took, and boom—there it is. Half my face just looks completely old and beat-up. I know my wife and the crew keep saying they barely notice a thing, but I'm not making it up from stress. \n\nIf my brain was gonna make me see things just from worrying, I'd be hallucinating about the price of building materials right now. You want to talk about worries, you should see the cost of plywood these days. It's highway robbery. We were framing out this custom build last month, and the supplier bumped the quote up by twenty percent overnight. The general contractor, Jimmy, he nearly blew a gasket. He was on the phone screaming at the rep for an hour straight. And don't even get me started on copper piping. Guys are breaking into active job sites just to strip the copper out of the walls and sell it for scrap. We had to hire a night watchman for a site downtown because they hit us three times in one week. Guy just sits in his truck drinking Mountain Dew all night, but I guess it keeps the thieves away. It's a crazy world right now. \n\nBut anyway... no, it's not my worries making the patch appear. I'm telling you, I can't stop seeing it. That patch is right there on my skin, making me look all beat-up, even if nobody else wants to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something that isn't actually there, but rather a product of your own mind?\n\nPatient: Doc, are we going in circles here? I feel like you're asking me the exact same thing again. Look, I know I've said it a hundred times, but it's not a product of my mind. I'm literally sitting in this office because I cannot stop looking at it. Every single time I pass a mirror or see a photo, my eyes go straight to that rough, sunken patch. It just makes that whole side of my face look so old and beat-up. I know my wife and the guys at the site swear they barely notice a thing, but I'm not inventing it. \n\nSpeaking of things that are just in people's minds, though, you should see some of the blueprints these architects send down to us. They sit in their air-conditioned offices and dream up these crazy designs that look great in their heads, but when you actually try to build it in the real world, the physics just don't work. We had this one guy last spring who wanted a solid marble kitchen island suspended from the ceiling with these thin little wire cables. Suspended! I told the GC, \"Unless you've got anti-gravity in the budget, that slab is coming straight down on someone's toes.\" We ended up having to weld this massive steel support structure hidden inside a fake drywall column just to make the guy's \"vision\" happen. Took an extra three weeks and cost the client an absolute fortune just because the architect wouldn't let go of this picture he had in his head. \n\nBut yeah... my face isn't like that. It's not some crazy imagination thing. It's real, and I can't stop seeing it, no matter how many times you ask me if it's all in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see the sunken patch on your face if a doctor or a dermatologist told you there was nothing wrong with your skin?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I haven't been to a skin doctor about it, so I'm not really sure what I'd do if they said that. I'd probably just think they needed to get their eyes checked, because I mean, I'm looking right at it in the mirror every day. \n\nSpeaking of skin doctors, though, my buddy Dave had to go to one last year. We were doing this massive gut rehab on a house from the seventies over in Westlake, and the attic was just packed to the rafters with that old, pink fiberglass insulation. Just nasty, itchy stuff. Well, Dave didn't wear his long sleeves like he was supposed to—he always runs hot, you know, sweats like a pig even in November. Anyway, he gets this fiberglass dust all in his pores, and his arms broke out in this insane, angry red rash. Looked like raw hamburger meat. He tried scrubbing it off in the utility sink with pumice soap, which obviously just made it ten times worse. He finally had to go to the dermatologist, and they gave him this heavy-duty steroid cream. Cost him like eighty bucks out of pocket even with the union insurance. Now the guy won't go near an attic without a full Tyvek suit. He looks like he's about to walk on the moon just to pull some drywall. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, if a doctor told me my face was fine. Yeah, I don't know. I'd probably just think they were blowing smoke, honestly. Because I can see it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, yeah, probably? I feel like we're beating a dead horse here, Doc. Even if a whole room full of doctors lined up and told me my skin was perfect, I'm the one who has to look in the mirror. I'm the one who sees the sunken, rough patch making me look all old and haggard. They can say whatever they want, but my eyes aren't broken.

You know, speaking of a bunch of doctors, my brother-in-law, Mike, he had to deal with that a few years back. He was having these crazy headaches, right? Just splitting migraines out of nowhere. He went to his regular doc, then a neurologist, then some kind of specialist up at the university hospital. Every single one of them told him he was fine, just stressed. They kept telling him to drink more water and take Advil. He went to like four different doctors, and they all said the same thing. Turns out, he had a slow carbon monoxide leak in his basement from a busted water heater. The doctors were all telling him there was nothing wrong with him, but the guy was slowly getting poisoned in his own house. If he hadn't had a plumber come out to fix a leaky pipe who happened to notice the venting was all rusted out, who knows what would have happened. 

So, yeah, doctors can be wrong. Even a bunch of them at once. If they told me my face was fine, I'd just think they were missing it, same as they missed Mike's carbon monoxide thing. Because the patch is there. I see it every single day.

It makes one side of me look completely old and beat-up. \n\nWhich, you know, it's funny about photos... we were taking a picture over at the job site the other day. We just finished framing this huge custom build out by the new highway. Took us forever because the lumber delivery got delayed twice. You wouldn't believe the supply chain issues we're still dealing with, it's driving my foreman absolutely crazy. Prices on plywood are just through the roof right now, too. \n\nBut anyway, I looked at that picture on my buddy's phone and that sunken patch was just glaring at me. Everyone—my wife, the guys at work—they keep saying they barely notice anything. They think I'm crazy. But I don't get how they don't see it. I was just checking it again in the rearview mirror of my truck in your parking lot... truck’s been acting up, actually. The transmission keeps slipping when I shift into second gear. Probably gonna cost me a fortune to rebuild it, and I just put new tires on the thing last month... but yeah, that's why I'm here. The face thing.\n\nDoctor: Is this sunken patch on your face the only thing you're concerned about when you look at yourself?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's pretty much just that patch. I mean, I'm 52, I don't expect to look like a kid anymore, so I'm not worried about my hair or my nose or anything like that. It's just that one rough, sunken spot. I stare at it every morning when I'm shaving... which has been a total nightmare lately, honestly, because we're right in the middle of redoing our master bathroom. \n\nMy wife wanted one of those double-sink vanities she saw on some home improvement show, so I spent my entire weekend tearing out the drywall and the old plumbing. And let me tell you, whoever owned the house before us did a complete hack job on the pipes behind the wall. They had PVC glued straight to copper, just a total disaster waiting to happen. I had to run to the hardware store like three times on Sunday just to get the right fittings to cap it off. The place was an absolute zoo, too, you can never find anyone in the plumbing aisle when you actually need them. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, I don't really focus on anything else when I look in the mirror. Just that one messed up area on my cheek.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that sunken patch on your face is actually there, or is it just something you see?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it has to be there, right? I see it plain as day. Though my wife swears it's just the lighting or my eyes playing tricks on me. \n\nWhich, speaking of lighting, I was just telling her that those new fixtures she picked out for the bathroom vanity are way too bright anyway. We got these LED bulbs, the daylight ones, you know? And I had to wire the whole thing myself because getting an electrician out to the house right now is just impossible. They want like two hundred bucks just to show up at the door! And don't even get me started on the price of Romex wire right now. I went to buy a 50-foot roll of 12/2 wire for that circuit and nearly had a heart attack right there in the aisle. It's like they think copper is made of gold these days. I ended up having to splice into the old junction box, which I hate doing, because you never know how the last guy pig-tailed the grounds, but... \n\nWait, what were you asking? Oh, if the spot is really there. I mean, yeah, I see it. Even if they don't, I know what I'm looking at. But I don't know, I guess maybe I just stare at it too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face isn't actually there, even if you see it?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. Maybe? I mean, I guess it's possible, since literally everyone else tells me I'm nuts. But it feels real to me. \n\nKind of like how my dog acts when the mailman comes by. We got this rescue a couple years ago, part shepherd, part who-knows-what. Great dog, mostly, but he goes absolutely ballistic every time the mail truck pulls up. He's convinced that guy is a threat, right? Barks his head off, jumping at the window. And I’m always telling him, \"Buddy, it's just Gary, he's bringing us junk mail and bills, he's not gonna hurt you.\" But to the dog, the threat is 100% real. He can't unsee it. \n\nWhich reminds me, I really need to fix the screen on that front window before summer. He clawed right through it last week when a squirrel ran across the porch railing. Those replacement screens aren't cheap either, and you have to get that little spline roller tool to push the rubber cord in... I tried doing it with a flathead screwdriver once and just ripped a hole right through the brand new mesh. So frustrating. \n\nBut yeah... what was the question? Oh, right. Is it possible it's not there. I mean, I suppose it's possible. It just doesn't feel that way when I'm looking right at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the sunken patch on your face at times when you're not looking in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. It's not just when I'm looking in the mirror. I mean, that's why I'm here, honestly. I just can't stop thinking about it. Even when I'm not looking at it, I'm picturing it. Like, I'll see a photo of myself and it's all I can focus on—this rough, sunken-looking patch that makes one side of my face look all old and beat-up. My wife and the guys at work keep telling me they barely notice anything, but it's always on my mind. \n\nIt actually distracted me at work the other day. We were pouring concrete for this new patio behind a house over in Westlake. You know, you really gotta pay attention when you're floating concrete, especially when the mix is a little wet and the sun is beating down on it. If you don't hit it with the bull float at the right time, the water bleeds to the top and you get this weak surface that's gonna flake off after the first freeze. Anyway, we had the truck backed right up to the forms, and the chute was swinging around... and I swear, I caught my reflection in the side mirror of the cement mixer. Just for a second. But it completely threw me off. \n\nI ended up stepping right into the wet pour. Ruined a good pair of boots, too. Those Red Wings aren't cheap, I've had them for three years and just got them resoled. The guy at the cobbler shop downtown did a great job, though. He used this heavy-duty Vibram sole, stitched it right on... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. It's always in the back of my head. Just picturing that beat-up spot on my face, even when I'm miles away from a mirror.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the sunken patch on your face affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. I mean, my wife is getting pretty fed up with me. I've been late getting out the door for work so many times because I'm just stuck in the bathroom, trying to fix how it looks. And you can't be late on a job site. The foreman, Dave, he's a real stickler for time. If you're not there by 6:00 AM sharp, ready to pull tools out of the truck, he'll just send you home for the day. No pay. And Dave's been on edge lately anyway because his truck's transmission is slipping. He's got this old Ford F-250, diesel, and he refuses to take it to a real mechanic. Says he can drop the pan and fix the valve body himself, but he's been saying that for six months. I keep telling him, \"Dave, once those clutch packs go, you're looking at a full rebuild.\" But he won't listen. \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, right, my family and stuff. Yeah, I've started dodging the site photos when the boss wants a picture of the crew in front of a finished pour. I just kind of step out of frame or pretend I need to grab something from the truck. And I've been skipping our family game nights on Fridays, too. My kids love playing Catan, but I just... I don't want to sit around the table under those bright lights with everyone looking at me. They think I'm just tired from work, but really I just don't want them staring at my face.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your work performance due to your concerns about the sunken patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying, it's definitely messing with work. I've been late getting out the door for jobs because I get stuck in the bathroom, just staring at it, trying to figure out if there's a way to hide it. And like I said, Dave doesn't mess around with guys being late. \n\nWhich, honestly, is ironic because he was late just yesterday. That transmission of his finally gave out on him on the highway. He had to get it towed all the way from the east side. The tow truck driver actually got lost, ended up in this residential neighborhood where the streets are too narrow, and he clipped somebody's mailbox. I guess it was one of those fancy brick ones, too, so that's gonna cost a fortune to fix. Dave was furious. He was pacing around the job site all morning, just chewing on a cigar he wasn't even smoking. \n\nBut, uh, yeah. Work. I've been dodging the site photos, too, like I mentioned. Whenever the boss wants a group shot of the crew in front of a finished pour, I just find an excuse to be somewhere else. I just don't want that patch on my face immortalized in some picture they're gonna put on the company Facebook page, you know? And it's not just work, it's home, too. Skipping those family game nights... I just don't want anyone looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the sunken patch on your face caused you to avoid social interactions or activities that you would normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I kind of already touched on that, didn't I? Like I was saying, I've been skipping out on our Friday family game nights. I just don't want to sit under that bright dining room chandelier with them staring at my face. We usually play Catan, or sometimes Monopoly. Though Monopoly always ends in an argument anyway because my youngest son always goes straight for the railroads. People think the expensive blue properties are the big ticket, but honestly, if you get all four railroads and the orange ones, you're set. Statistically, people land on the orange ones the most because of where they are in relation to the jail space. I actually read this whole article about it in a magazine while I was waiting at the dentist a few years ago. Dr. Henderson, great guy. He retired last spring, though, and sold his practice to this young kid right out of dental school who tried to tell me I needed an expensive night guard... \n\nAnyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, avoiding things. Yeah, I skip the game nights now. And like I mentioned before, I'm still dodging those site photos at work, and I'm running late in the mornings getting out the door for jobs because I'm stuck in the mirror. I just can't stand the idea of people looking at that sunken patch, so I just try to keep out of the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concern about the sunken patch on your face is affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it is. I mean, it's hard to be happy when you're just constantly worried about it. Like I've been saying, it's messing with everything. I'm late for jobs because I'm stuck in the bathroom, I'm dodging the crew photos, and I'm skipping game nights with the kids. It just... it wears you down. \n\nAnd speaking of wearing down, my work boots are completely shot. I've had these Red Wings for about four years now, and the tread is just gone. I almost slipped on a patch of wet concrete the other day. You gotta be careful with wet concrete, too, because if it gets on your skin and you don't wash it off right away, it'll give you chemical burns. We had a new guy last summer, barely eighteen, who didn't listen when we told him to wear long pants. He ended up with these nasty red welts all over his shins. Boss had to send him to the clinic. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. It just feels like this whole thing with my face is taking over. I'm just so tired of trying to hide it all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your perception of the sunken patch on your face might be different from how it actually looks to others?\n\nPatient: I mean... yeah, I guess so. People keep telling me that, anyway. My wife, the guys at the site, they all keep saying they barely notice anything at all. But I just don't get how they can't see it. I look in the mirror, or I look at those photos, and I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch. It makes that whole side of me look so old and beat-up. \n\nSpeaking of looking old, I actually thought maybe it was just bad lighting casting a weird shadow, so I tried changing the bulbs in our bathroom vanity last weekend. I went down to Home Depot to get those soft white ones, but the lighting aisle is a total mess now. It's all LEDs. You can't even find a normal incandescent bulb anymore because of those new energy regulations. I get wanting to save electricity, I really do, but those LEDs give off this weird, harsh light, like you're in a hospital or a meat cooler or something. I spent twenty minutes just staring at the boxes trying to figure out the lumens and the kelvins. The guy working there, kid with a green apron, didn't know anything either. He was just stocking the dimmer switches and looking at his phone. \n\nBut anyway... what was I saying? Oh, right. Even with the new bulbs, I still see it. It's just right there, making my face look all caved in, even if everyone else swears they have no idea what I'm talking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is only visible to you?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know how it could be *only* visible to me. It's right there. Like I said, I'm here because I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face. Every time I look in the mirror, or if I accidentally catch a glimpse of myself in one of those photos, it's all I can focus on. It makes that whole side of my face look so old and beat-up. \n\nIt's like when you buy a used truck, you know? My buddy Dave just bought this '08 Chevy Silverado. He thought he got a steal on it, but the guy who sold it to him painted over a massive rust spot on the rear quarter panel. Dave didn't notice it until it rained a few days later and the cheap paint started flaking off. Now he's got this huge rusted-out crater on the side of his truck, and he's furious. He's talking about taking the guy to small claims court, but I told him, \"Dave, you bought it as-is off Craigslist. Good luck with that.\" You really have to check the undercarriage, too, when you buy used. People forget that, but that's where the salt from the roads really eats away at the frame during the winter. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... the patch on my face. It's like that rust spot, you know? It's just glaring at me. Even though my wife and the guys at work keep saying they barely notice anything, I just don't understand how they can't see it. It's right there. I see it every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something that only you can see, and it's not actually there at all?\n\nPatient: Not there at all? I just... I don't see how that's possible. It's not like I'm imagining things. It's right there on my skin. It's not a mirage. \n\nYou know, speaking of mirages, it gets exactly like that when we do road work in the dead of summer. The asphalt gets so hot it literally looks like there are puddles of water on the highway ahead of you. We were doing this long stretch of I-95 a couple years back, and the heat index was pushing a hundred and ten. You have to drink those electrolyte powders they hand out, or you will absolutely drop. We had this foreman, Gary, stubborn old guy who insisted he only needed black coffee and cigarettes to get through a twelve-hour shift. Well, around two in the afternoon, he just keeled over right next to the steam roller. Scared the absolute hell out of us. The paramedics said his core temp was dangerously high. Ever since then, the safety coordinator makes us take mandatory water and shade breaks every hour when it gets above ninety degrees. It slows down the whole project, but I guess it beats having guys pass out on the blacktop. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I am not hallucinating this thing on my face. It is real. Even if everyone else is blind to it or just trying to make me feel better, I know what I am looking at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something you're seeing because of your own worries, rather than something that's actually there?\n\nPatient: Look, Doc, I feel like a broken record here. It's not just my anxiety playing tricks on me. Like I keep trying to explain, the whole reason I'm even sitting in this chair is because I cannot stop obsessing over that rough, sunken spot. I look in the mirror, or I see a picture somebody took, and boom—there it is. Half my face just looks completely old and beat-up. I know my wife and the crew keep saying they barely notice a thing, but I'm not making it up from stress. \n\nIf my brain was gonna make me see things just from worrying, I'd be hallucinating about the price of building materials right now. You want to talk about worries, you should see the cost of plywood these days. It's highway robbery. We were framing out this custom build last month, and the supplier bumped the quote up by twenty percent overnight. The general contractor, Jimmy, he nearly blew a gasket. He was on the phone screaming at the rep for an hour straight. And don't even get me started on copper piping. Guys are breaking into active job sites just to strip the copper out of the walls and sell it for scrap. We had to hire a night watchman for a site downtown because they hit us three times in one week. Guy just sits in his truck drinking Mountain Dew all night, but I guess it keeps the thieves away. It's a crazy world right now. \n\nBut anyway... no, it's not my worries making the patch appear. I'm telling you, I can't stop seeing it. That patch is right there on my skin, making me look all beat-up, even if nobody else wants to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something that isn't actually there, but rather a product of your own mind?\n\nPatient: Doc, are we going in circles here? I feel like you're asking me the exact same thing again. Look, I know I've said it a hundred times, but it's not a product of my mind. I'm literally sitting in this office because I cannot stop looking at it. Every single time I pass a mirror or see a photo, my eyes go straight to that rough, sunken patch. It just makes that whole side of my face look so old and beat-up. I know my wife and the guys at the site swear they barely notice a thing, but I'm not inventing it. \n\nSpeaking of things that are just in people's minds, though, you should see some of the blueprints these architects send down to us. They sit in their air-conditioned offices and dream up these crazy designs that look great in their heads, but when you actually try to build it in the real world, the physics just don't work. We had this one guy last spring who wanted a solid marble kitchen island suspended from the ceiling with these thin little wire cables. Suspended! I told the GC, \"Unless you've got anti-gravity in the budget, that slab is coming straight down on someone's toes.\" We ended up having to weld this massive steel support structure hidden inside a fake drywall column just to make the guy's \"vision\" happen. Took an extra three weeks and cost the client an absolute fortune just because the architect wouldn't let go of this picture he had in his head. \n\nBut yeah... my face isn't like that. It's not some crazy imagination thing. It's real, and I can't stop seeing it, no matter how many times you ask me if it's all in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see the sunken patch on your face if a doctor or a dermatologist told you there was nothing wrong with your skin?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I haven't been to a skin doctor about it, so I'm not really sure what I'd do if they said that. I'd probably just think they needed to get their eyes checked, because I mean, I'm looking right at it in the mirror every day. \n\nSpeaking of skin doctors, though, my buddy Dave had to go to one last year. We were doing this massive gut rehab on a house from the seventies over in Westlake, and the attic was just packed to the rafters with that old, pink fiberglass insulation. Just nasty, itchy stuff. Well, Dave didn't wear his long sleeves like he was supposed to—he always runs hot, you know, sweats like a pig even in November. Anyway, he gets this fiberglass dust all in his pores, and his arms broke out in this insane, angry red rash. Looked like raw hamburger meat. He tried scrubbing it off in the utility sink with pumice soap, which obviously just made it ten times worse. He finally had to go to the dermatologist, and they gave him this heavy-duty steroid cream. Cost him like eighty bucks out of pocket even with the union insurance. Now the guy won't go near an attic without a full Tyvek suit. He looks like he's about to walk on the moon just to pull some drywall. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, if a doctor told me my face was fine. Yeah, I don't know. I'd probably just think they were blowing smoke, honestly. Because I can see it.\n\nDoctor: Would you still believe the sunken patch is on your face even if multiple doctors told you there's nothing wrong with your skin?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, probably? I feel like we're beating a dead horse here, Doc. Even if a whole room full of doctors lined up and told me my skin was perfect, I'm the one who has to look in the mirror. I'm the one who sees the sunken, rough patch making me look all old and haggard. They can say whatever they want, but my eyes aren't broken.\n\nYou know, speaking of a bunch of doctors, my brother-in-law, Mike, he had to deal with that a few years back. He was having these crazy headaches, right? Just splitting migraines out of nowhere. He went to his regular doc, then a neurologist, then some kind of specialist up at the university hospital. Every single one of them told him he was fine, just stressed. They kept telling him to drink more water and take Advil. He went to like four different doctors, and they all said the same thing. Turns out, he had a slow carbon monoxide leak in his basement from a busted water heater. The doctors were all telling him there was nothing wrong with him, but the guy was slowly getting poisoned in his own house. If he hadn't had a plumber come out to fix a leaky pipe who happened to notice the venting was all rusted out, who knows what would have happened. \n\nSo, yeah, doctors can be wrong. Even a bunch of them at once. If they told me my face was fine, I'd just think they were missing it, same as they missed Mike's carbon monoxide thing. Because the patch is there. I see it every single day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I just... no. I don't see how I could be mistaken. I mean, I'm looking right at it. It's not like a trick of the light or something. It's my face. 

Mistakes happen, sure. God knows I've seen enough mistakes on the job site to last a lifetime. Just last week, the framing crew—bunch of young kids, mostly—they framed out this entire master bathroom on the second floor of a custom build, right? They had the plans, they had the measurements, everything. But somebody read the tape measure wrong, and they framed the rough opening for the shower stall two inches too narrow. Two inches! Doesn't sound like a lot, but when the custom glass enclosure arrived from Italy or wherever the hell the designer ordered it from, it didn't fit. You can't just shave down tempered glass. So the whole crew had to come in on a Saturday, rip out the drywall, re-frame the wall, and re-mud the whole thing. The foreman was screaming his head off, throwing his hard hat... it was a complete circus. All because one kid couldn't read a tape measure right. 

But, like, my face isn't a tape measure. I'm not misreading it. I wake up, I look in the mirror, and the rough, sunken patch is right there staring back at me. I'm not mistaken about it. It's just... there.

Well, Dave didn't wear his long sleeves like he was supposed to—he always runs hot, you know, sweats like a pig even in November. Oh, right, if a doctor told me my face was fine. Yeah, I don't know. I'd probably just think they were blowing smoke, honestly. The doctors were all telling him there was nothing wrong with him, but the guy was slowly getting poisoned in his own house. If he hadn't had a plumber come out to fix a leaky pipe who happened to notice the venting was all rusted out, who knows what would have happened. \n\nSo, yeah, doctors can be wrong. Even a bunch of them at once. If they told me my face was fine, I'd just think they were missing it, same as they missed Mike's carbon monoxide thing. Because the patch is there. It's just... there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. They can say whatever they want, but my eyes aren't broken.\n\nYou know, speaking of a bunch of doctors, my brother-in-law, Mike, he had to deal with that a few years back. Every single one of them told him he was fine, just stressed. They kept telling him to drink more water and take Advil. He went to like four different doctors, and they all said the same thing. Turns out, he had a slow carbon monoxide leak in his basement from a busted water heater. The doctors were all telling him there was nothing wrong with him, but the guy was slowly getting poisoned in his own house. I'm not misreading it. I wake up, I look in the mirror, and the rough, sunken patch is right there staring back at me. I'm not mistaken about it. It's just... there.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you could be convinced that the sunken patch is not there if I showed you a picture of yourself where it's not visible?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A picture? I mean... no, probably not. I'd just think the lighting was washing it out or something. Cameras don't always show what's really there, you know? 

Like, speaking of cameras, you wouldn't believe what the general contractor brought to the site a couple weeks ago. This massive, high-tech drone. They don't even send the safety guys up on the scaffolding to check the masonry pointing anymore, they just fly this loud, buzzing thing right outside the safety netting. Anyway, the drone takes all these high-res photos of the east wall, and the site boss comes down screaming that we missed a whole section of mortar on the fourth floor. Just chewing us out. So me and my foreman climb all the way up there, and the mortar is fine. It's perfect. The sun was just hitting the brick at this weird angle because it was late in the afternoon, and the camera made it look like a deep shadow, like a big hole in the wall. Cost us like three hours of arguing with the architect over a trick of the light. 

So, yeah... what were we saying? Oh, a picture of me. Yeah, if you showed me a photo where my face looked normal, I'd just think it was like that drone picture. Just the flash or the light hitting it wrong and hiding the sunken part. Because I know what I see in the mirror. It's definitely there.

People keep telling me that, anyway. My wife, the guys at the site, they all keep saying they barely notice anything at all. But I just don't get how they can't see it. I look in the mirror, or I look at those photos, and I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch. It makes that whole side of me look so old and beat-up. \n\nSpeaking of looking old, I actually thought maybe it was just bad lighting casting a weird shadow, so I tried changing the bulbs in our bathroom vanity last weekend. I went down to Home Depot to get those soft white ones, but the lighting aisle is a total mess now. It's all LEDs. You can't even find a normal incandescent bulb anymore because of those new energy regulations. It's right there. Like I said, I'm here because I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face. Every time I look in the mirror, or if I accidentally catch a glimpse of myself in one of those photos, it's all I can focus on. It makes that whole side of my face look so old and beat-up. \n\nIt's like when you buy a used truck, you know? My buddy Dave just bought this '08 Chevy Silverado. He thought he got a steal on it, but the guy who sold it to him painted over a massive rust spot on the rear quarter panel. Dave didn't notice it until it rained a few days later and the cheap paint started flaking off. Now he's got this huge rusted-out crater on the side of his truck, and he's furious. He's talking about taking the guy to small claims court, but I told him, \"Dave, you bought it as-is off Craigslist. Good luck with that.\" You really have to check the undercarriage, too, when you buy used. People forget that, but that's where the salt from the roads really eats away at the frame during the winter. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... the patch on my face. It's like that rust spot, you know? It's just glaring at me. Even though my wife and the guys at work keep saying they barely notice anything, I just don't understand how they can't see it. It's right there. I see it every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something that only you can see, and it's not actually there at all?\n\nPatient: Not there at all? I just... I don't see how that's possible. It's not like I'm imagining things. It's right there on my skin. It's not a mirage. \n\nYou know, speaking of mirages, it gets exactly like that when we do road work in the dead of summer. The asphalt gets so hot it literally looks like there are puddles of water on the highway ahead of you. We were doing this long stretch of I-95 a couple years back, and the heat index was pushing a hundred and ten. You have to drink those electrolyte powders they hand out, or you will absolutely drop. We had this foreman, Gary, stubborn old guy who insisted he only needed black coffee and cigarettes to get through a twelve-hour shift. Well, around two in the afternoon, he just keeled over right next to the steam roller. Scared the absolute hell out of us. The paramedics said his core temp was dangerously high. Ever since then, the safety coordinator makes us take mandatory water and shade breaks every hour when it gets above ninety degrees. It slows down the whole project, but I guess it beats having guys pass out on the blacktop. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I am not hallucinating this thing on my face. It is real. Even if everyone else is blind to it or just trying to make me feel better, I know what I am looking at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something you're seeing because of your own worries, rather than something that's actually there?\n\nPatient: Look, Doc, I feel like a broken record here. It's not just my anxiety playing tricks on me. Like I keep trying to explain, the whole reason I'm even sitting in this chair is because I cannot stop obsessing over that rough, sunken spot. I look in the mirror, or I see a picture somebody took, and boom—there it is. Half my face just looks completely old and beat-up. I know my wife and the crew keep saying they barely notice a thing, but I'm not making it up from stress. \n\nIf my brain was gonna make me see things just from worrying, I'd be hallucinating about the price of building materials right now. You want to talk about worries, you should see the cost of plywood these days. It's highway robbery. We were framing out this custom build last month, and the supplier bumped the quote up by twenty percent overnight. The general contractor, Jimmy, he nearly blew a gasket. He was on the phone screaming at the rep for an hour straight. And don't even get me started on copper piping. Guys are breaking into active job sites just to strip the copper out of the walls and sell it for scrap. We had to hire a night watchman for a site downtown because they hit us three times in one week. Guy just sits in his truck drinking Mountain Dew all night, but I guess it keeps the thieves away. It's a crazy world right now. \n\nBut anyway... no, it's not my worries making the patch appear. I'm telling you, I can't stop seeing it. That patch is right there on my skin, making me look all beat-up, even if nobody else wants to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the sunken patch on your face is something that isn't actually there, but rather a product of your own mind?\n\nPatient: Doc, are we going in circles here? I feel like you're asking me the exact same thing again. Look, I know I've said it a hundred times, but it's not a product of my mind. I'm literally sitting in this office because I cannot stop looking at it. Every single time I pass a mirror or see a photo, my eyes go straight to that rough, sunken patch. It just makes that whole side of my face look so old and beat-up. Took an extra three weeks and cost the client an absolute fortune just because the architect wouldn't let go of this picture he had in his head. \n\nBut yeah... my face isn't like that. It's not some crazy imagination thing. I haven't been to a skin doctor about it, so I'm not really sure what I'd do if they said that. I'd probably just think they needed to get their eyes checked, because I mean, I'm looking right at it in the mirror every day. \n\nSpeaking of skin doctors, though, my buddy Dave had to go to one last year. Well, Dave didn't wear his long sleeves like he was supposed to—he always runs hot, you know, sweats like a pig even in November. He tried scrubbing it off in the utility sink with pumice soap, which obviously just made it ten times worse. He finally had to go to the dermatologist, and they gave him this heavy-duty steroid cream. Cost him like eighty bucks out of pocket even with the union insurance. Now the guy won't go near an attic without a full Tyvek suit. He looks like he's about to walk on the moon just to pull some drywall. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Because I can see it.\n\nDoctor: Would you still believe the sunken patch is on your face even if multiple doctors told you there's nothing wrong with your skin?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, probably? I feel like we're beating a dead horse here, Doc. Even if a whole room full of doctors lined up and told me my skin was perfect, I'm the one who has to look in the mirror. I'm the one who sees the sunken, rough patch making me look all old and haggard. They can say whatever they want, but my eyes aren't broken.\n\nYou know, speaking of a bunch of doctors, my brother-in-law, Mike, he had to deal with that a few years back.
